Abstract. Models are widely used to research hydrological            1   Introduction change and risk. However, the power embedded in the mod- elling process and outcomes is often concealed by claiming           Water flows and storages are increasingly researched and their neutrality. Our review shows that in the scientific litera-    governed through quantitative (hydrological, hydrodynamic, ture relatively little attention is given to the influence of mod-   socio-hydrological, and hydro-economic) models. These els on development processes and outcomes in water gover-            models are used with different purposes, including docu- nance. At the same time, an emerging body of work offering           menting water distribution, exploring causal dynamics, sim- critical insights into the political implications of hydrologi-      ulating changes, predicting future conditions, and inform- cal models and a nuanced understanding of their application          ing policy-making. Far from being neutral tools, models are in context has begun to flourish. Drawing on this work, we           shaped by policy projects, institutional backgrounds, spe- call for power-sensitive modelling which includes the follow-        cific traditions and practices of modellers, and gendered rela- ing considerations: take a holistic approach to modelling be-        tions and experiences (Sismondo, 1999; Knorr-Cetina, 1999; yond programming and coding; foster accountability; work             Lane, 2012; MacKenzie, 2006; Melsen et al., 2018a; Ad- towards just and equitable water distributions; be transparent       dor and Melsen, 2019). Since models are complex and the about the expectations and choices made; and democratise             places and people that develop a model may be disconnected modelling by giving space to and being mindful of represen-          from the places and people that use the model, unravelling tations of multiple bodies of knowledge and multiple stake-          how and why a model functions, and with what influence, is holders and by incorporating marginalised people and nature          complicated (Kouw, 2016). However, we argue in this paper into the modelling process. Our call should not be understood        that this complexity is an often-missed piece of the puzzle as a suggestion to do away with modelling altogether, but            in model commission and development, and consciously en- rather as an invitation to interrogate how quantitative mod-         gaging with it can help to improve the models’ fit for purpose els may help to foster transformative pathways towards more          or support a modelling process that contributes towards more just and equitable water distributions.                              just and equitable water distributions. Models are not neutral, and those who commission and de- velop models do have choices in whether modelling should [SOURCE: Page_2]
